Density Hajnal–Szemerédi theorem for cliques of size four
Jianfeng Hou, Caiyun Hu, Xizhi Liu, Yixiao Zhang

Abstract
The celebrated Corrádi–Hajnal Theorem [ CH63 ] and the Hajnal–Szemerédi Theorem [ HS70 ] determined the exact minimum degree thresholds for a graph on n n vertices to contain k k vertex-disjoint copies of K r K_{r} , for r = 3 r=3 and general r ≥ 4 r\geq 4 , respectively. The edge density version of the Corrádi–Hajnal Theorem was established by Allen–Böttcher–Hladký–Piguet [ ABHP15 ] for large n n . Remarkably, they determined the four classes of extremal constructions corresponding to different intervals of k k . They further proposed the natural problem of establishing a density version of the Hajnal–Szemerédi Theorem: For r ≥ 4 r\geq 4 , what is the edge density threshold that guarantees a graph on n n vertices contains k k vertex-disjoint copies of K r K_{r} for k ≤ n / r k\leq n/r . They also remarked, “We are not even sure what the complete family of extremal graphs should be.” We take the first step toward this problem by determining asymptotically the five classes of extremal constructions for r = 4 r=4 . Furthermore, we propose a candidate set comprising r + 1 r+1 classes of extremal constructions for general r ≥ 5 r\geq 5 .



1
Introduction

1.1
Motivation and main results

We identify a graph
G
with its edge set; in particular,
|G|
represents the number of edges in
G
.
The number of vertices in
G
is denoted by
v(G)
.
The
minimum degree
,
average degree
, and
maximum degree
of
G
are denoted by
\delta(G)
,
d(G)
, and
\Delta(G)
, respectively.

Given two graphs
F
and
G
, a collection
\mathcal{C}
of vertex-disjoint copies of
F
in
G
is called an
F
-tiling
in
G
. We denote by
V(\mathcal{C})
the set of vertices covered by the members of
\mathcal{C}
.
The
F
-matching number

\nu(F,G)
of
G
is the maximum size of an
F
-tiling in
G
.
The case
F=K_{2}
corresponds to the well-known
matching number
of
G
, which is denoted simply as
\nu(G)
.
We say a graph
G
is
F
-free
if
\nu(F,G)=0
.

The study of the following problem encompasses several central topics in Extremal Graph Theory.
Given a graph
F
and integers
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
n\geq k\cdot v(F)\geq 0
:

What constraints on an
n
-vertex graph
G
force it to satisfy
\nu(F,G)\geq k+1
?

(
\ast
)

From the perspective of the minimum degree, the celebrated Corrádi–Hajnal Theorem
[
CH63
]
and Hajnal–Szemerédi Theorem
[
HS70
]
determined the precise minimum degree threshold that ensures a graph
G
satisfies
\nu(K_{r},G)\geq k+1
for
r=3
and
r\geq 4
, respectively.
These two foundational results have inspired numerous subsequent works (see e.g.
[
Sey73
,
FH94
,
FK95
,
KSS96
,
FK96a
,
FK96b
,
AY96
,
KSS98a
,
KSS98b
,
Kom00
,
KSS01
,
Kaw02
,
SZ03
,
SZ04
,
KO06
,
CKO07
,
KK08
,
KO09b
,
BST09
,
Zha09
,
HS10
,
ABH11
,
KM15
,
Tre16
,
MS17
,
HT20
,
LSM23
]
) and have been extended to general graphs.
For additional related results, we refer the reader to surveys
[
KS96
,
KSSS02
,
KO09a
]
.

Theorem 1.1

(Corrádi–Hajnal
[
CH63
]
, Hajnal–Szemerédi
[
HS70
]
)
.

Let
r\geq 3
and
n\geq r(k+1)\geq 0
be integers.
Suppose that

\displaystyle\delta(G)>k+\left\lfloor\frac{r-2}{r-1}(n-k)\right\rfloor.

Then
\nu(K_{r},G)\geq k+1
.

Remark
. The tightness of the bound
\delta(G)>k+\left\lfloor\frac{r-2}{r-1}(n-k)\right\rfloor
is demonstrated by the construction
E_{1}(n,k,r)
defined in Section
8
.

From the perspective of the average degree, the well-studied Turán problem (see e.g.
[
Man07
,
Tur41
,
ES46
,
FS13
]
), which asks for the maximum number
\mathrm{ex}(n,F)
of edges in an
F
-free graph on
n
vertices, is equivalent to determining the minimum edge density of
G
that ensures
\nu(F,G)\geq 1
.
The celebrated Erdős–Gallai Theorem
[
EG59
]
determined for all integers
n\geq 2t\geq 0
, the minimum edge density of
G
that ensures
\nu(G)\geq t
.
For general graphs, the answer is less complete.
For convenience, let
\mathrm{ex}\left(n,(k+1)F\right)
denote the maximum number of edges in an
n
-vertex graph
G
that satisfies
\nu(F,G)<k+1
.
An old theorem by Erdős
[
Erd62a
]
determined
\mathrm{ex}\left(n,(k+1)K_{3}\right)
for
k\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}}{20}
.
Later, Moon
[
Moo68
]
improved and extended the theorem of Erdős to general
r\geq 4
and determined
\mathrm{ex}\left(n,(k+1)K_{r}\right)
for
k\leq\frac{2n-3r^{2}+8r-5}{r^{3}-r^{2}+1}
.
Akiyama–Frankl
[
AF85
]
determined
\mathrm{ex}\left(n,(k+1)K_{r}\right)
for the case when
k=\lfloor n/r\rfloor-1
(see also
[
BE78
]
), which was later extended by
[
BKT13
]
.
For bipartite graphs
F
, Grosu–Hladký
[
GH12
]
establihsed a general upper bound for
\mathrm{ex}\left(n,(k+1)F\right)
, which is asymptotically tight for certain classes of bipartite graphs.
Extending the work of Erdős and Moon, we established general upper bounds, which are tight in many cases, for
\mathrm{ex}\left(n,(k+1)F\right)
in a sequence of studies
[
HLL+23
,
HHL+23b
,
HHL+23a
,
HHL+24
]
, when
k
lies in the interval
\left[0,\varepsilon_{F}\frac{\mathrm{ex}(n,F)}{n}\right]
, where
\varepsilon_{F}>0
is a small constant.

Determining
\mathrm{ex}\left(n,(k+1)F\right)
, even asymptotically, for all
k
in the interval
\left[0,\frac{n}{v(F)}\right]
is extremely challenging in general.
Indeed, even in the case where
F
is a triangle,
\mathrm{ex}\left(n,(k+1)F\right)
was completely determined only a decade ago by Allen–Böttcher–Hladký–Piguet
[
ABHP15
]
for large
n
.
Their results, which we find remarkable, show that there are four different classes of extremal constructions corresponding to four different regimes of
k
.
Below, we present only the asymptotic version of their result and refer the reader to
[
ABHP15
]
for additional details on the extremal constructions.

Theorem 1.2

(Allen–Böttcher–Hladký–Piguet
[
ABHP15
]
)
.

Suppose that
n
is sufficiently large. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_{3})=O(n)+\begin{cases}\frac{n^{2}}{4}+\frac{kn}{2}-\frac{k^{2}}{4},&\quad\text{if}\quad k\in\left[0,\frac{2n}{9}\right],\\[5.0pt]
\frac{n^{2}}{4}+2k^{2},&\quad\text{if}\quad k\in\left[\frac{2n}{9},\frac{n}{4}\right],\\[5.0pt]
2kn-2k^{2},&\quad\text{if}\quad k\in\left[\frac{n}{4},\frac{(5+\sqrt{3})n}{22}\right],\\[5.0pt]
\frac{n^{2}}{2}-3kn+9k^{2},&\quad\text{if}\quad k\in\left[\frac{(5+\sqrt{3})n}{22},\frac{n}{3}\right].\end{cases}

In
[
ABHP15
]
, Allen–Böttcher–Hladký–Piguet raised the problem of establishing a density version of the Hajnal–Szemerédi Theorme, i.e. determining
\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_{r})
for
r\geq 4
and
k\in\left[0,\frac{n}{r}\right]
.
They remarked:

Some parts of such an argument can be made to work, but there are some additional difficulties for
r\geq 4
that do not appear for
r=3
. We are not even sure what the complete family of extremal graphs should be.

We contribute to this problem by proposing a candidate set of extremal constructions for general
r\geq 4
(see Section
8
), and determining asymptotically the value of
\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_{r})
for all
k\in\left[0,\frac{n}{4}\right]
.

0
\frac{2}{13}
\frac{1}{6}
\frac{4-\sqrt{2}}{14}
\frac{11+\sqrt{7}}{57}
\frac{1}{4}
\frac{1}{3}
\frac{1}{2}
\frac{k}{n}
\frac{\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_{4})}{n^{2}}

E_{2}

Figure 1:
The asymptotic behavior of
\frac{\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_{4})}{n^{2}}
as a function of
\frac{k}{n}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose that
n
and
k
are integers satisfying
n\geq 4k\geq 0
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_{4})=\Xi(n,k)+O(n),

where

\displaystyle\Xi(n,k)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{n^{2}}{3}+\frac{kn}{3}-\frac{k^{2}}{6},&\quad\text{if}\quad k\in\left[0,\frac{2n}{13}\right],\\[5.0pt]
\frac{n^{2}}{3}+2k^{2},&\quad\text{if}\quad k\in\left[\frac{2n}{13},\frac{n}{6}\right],\\[5.0pt]
\frac{n^{2}}{4}+kn-k^{2},&\quad\text{if}\quad k\in\left[\frac{n}{6},\frac{(4-\sqrt{2})n}{14}\right],\\[5.0pt]
3kn-\frac{9k^{2}}{2},&\quad\text{if}\quad k\in\left[\frac{(4-\sqrt{2})n}{14},\frac{(11+\sqrt{7})n}{57}\right],\\[5.0pt]
n^{2}-8kn+24k^{2},&\quad\text{if}\quad k\in\left[\frac{(11+\sqrt{7})n}{57},\frac{n}{4}\right].\end{cases}

Remarks.

(i)

The function
\Xi(n,k)
arises from the edge densities of five classes of constructions
E_{1}(n,k),\ldots,E_{5}(n,k)
, which we will define in the next subsection.
We conjecture that these constructions are extremal, i.e.
\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_{4})=\max_{i\in[5]}|E_{i}(n,k)|
, when
n
is large.
Our current method can be modified to prove this conjecture when
k
lies in the intervals
\left[0,\frac{(20+\sqrt{10})n}{130}\right]\cup\left[\frac{n}{5},\frac{n}{4}\right]
.
However, doing so requires extensive case analysis and very technical details, which we have chosen not to present here.

(ii)

Another potential approach that we believe could be useful (although it may also require significant technical work) for determining the exact value of
\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_{4})
, is the stability method, first introduced by Simonovits
[
Sim68
]
.
Therefore, we include a stability version of Theorem
1.3
below for further exploration in this direction.

Given two graphs
G
and
H
with the same number of vertices, the
edit distance

\mathrm{edit}(G,H)
between
G
and
H
is the minimum number of edge additions and deletions required to transform
G
into a copy of
H
.

Theorem 1.4
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta>0
and
N_{0}
such that the following holds for all
n\geq N_{0}
.
Let
i\in[5]
and
k\in I_{i}(n)
(as defined later in Table
1
). Suppose that
G
is an
n
-vertex graph satisfying
\nu(K_{4},G)\leq k
and
|G|\geq(1-\delta)\cdot\Xi(n,k)
. Then
\mathrm{edit}(G,E_{i}(n,k))\leq\varepsilon n^{2}
.

Theorem
1.4
follows from the proof of Theorem
1.3
with straightforward modifications, and the tedious details are omitted here.

Other related work
:
Note that the edge density version of Question (

∗

‣
1.1
) can be rephrased as follows:

Given edge density, minimize the number of vertex-disjoint copies of
F
in a graph.

(
\star
)

One could replace “the number of vertex-disjoint copies of
F
” with other parameters, such as “the number of copies of
F
”, which corresponds to the classical Erdős–Rademacher Problem (see
[
Erd55
,
Goo59
,
Erd62b
,
MM62
,
NS63
,
Bol76
,
LS76
,
Nik77
,
NK81
,
LS83
,
Fis89
,
Raz08
,
Nik11
,
Rei16
,
Mub10
,
Mub13
,
PR17
,
KLPS20
,
LPS20
,
LM22
,
BC23
,
MY23
,
LP25
]
for related results); or with “the number of edge-disjoint copies of
F
” (see e.g.
[
EGP66
,
Erd71
,
Győ88
,
Győ91
]
for related results).
These results, combined with theorems from Extremal Set Theory (see the survey
[
FT16
]
), can provide some bounds for (

⋆

‣
1.1
). However, these bounds are usually non-tight (even asymptotically), as the extremal constructions for these problems are usually quite different.

1.2
Extremal constructions

In this subsection, we describe the structures of the five classes
E_{1}(n,k),\ldots,E_{5}(n,k)
of extremal constructions.
For convenience, we slightly abuse notation by referring to
E_{i}(n,k)
as a typical member in this class.

Given
t
pairwise disjoint sets
V_{1},\ldots,V_{t}
, we use
K_{t}[V_{1},\ldots,V_{t}]
to denote
the complete
t
-partite graph
with parts
V_{1},\ldots,V_{t}
. We will omit the subscript
t
if it is clear from the context.

E_{1}(n,k)
X
Y_{1}
Y_{2}
Y_{3}
X
E_{4}(n,k)
Y_{1}
X
E_{5}(n,k)
Y_{1}
Y_{2}
Y_{3}
Y_{4}
Y_{5}
Y_{6}
X
E_{3}(n,k)
Y_{1}
Y_{2}
Y_{1}
Y_{2}
Y_{3}
E_{2}(n,k)
X

Figure 2:
Structures of
E_{1}(n,k),\ldots,E_{5}(n,k)
.

Let
n\geq 4k\geq 0
be integers. Define the following five classes of graphs on
n
vertices (see Figure
2
)
\colon

•

The vertex set of
E_{1}(n,k)
consists of four parts
X,Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}
, with sizes given by

\displaystyle|X|=k,\quad|Y_{1}|=\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{3}\right\rfloor,\quad|Y_{2}|=\left\lfloor\frac{n-k+1}{3}\right\rfloor,\quad\text{and}\quad|Y_{3}|=\left\lfloor\frac{n-k+2}{3}\right\rfloor.

The edge set of
E_{1}(n,k)
is defined as

\displaystyle E_{1}(n,k)=\binom{X}{2}\cup K[X,Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}].

•

The vertex set of
E_{2}(n,k)
consists of four parts
X,Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}
, with sizes satisfying

\displaystyle|X|=2k+1,\quad|Y_{1}|=\left\lfloor\frac{n+i_{1}}{3}\right\rfloor,\quad|Y_{2}|=\left\lfloor\frac{n+i_{2}}{3}\right\rfloor,\quad\text{and}\quad|Y_{3}|=\left\lfloor\frac{n+i_{3}}{3}\right\rfloor-|X|,

where
\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}=\{0,1,2\}
.
The edge set of
E_{2}(n,k)
is defined as

\displaystyle E_{2}(n,k)=\binom{X}{2}\cup K[X\cup Y_{3},Y_{1},Y_{2}].

Note that
E_{2}(n,k)
is defined only for
k\in\left[0,\frac{n-1}{6}\right]
.

•

The vertex set of
E_{3}(n,k)
consists of three parts
X,Y_{1},Y_{2}
, with sizes given by

\displaystyle|X|=2k+1,\quad|Y_{1}|=\left\lfloor\frac{n-|X|}{2}\right\rfloor,\quad\text{and}\quad|Y_{2}|=\left\lceil\frac{n-|X|}{2}\right\rceil.

The edge set of
E_{3}(n,k)
is defined as

\displaystyle E_{3}(n,k)=\binom{X}{2}\cup K[X,Y_{1},Y_{2}].

•

The vertex set of
E_{4}(n,k)
consists of two parts
X,Y_{1}
, with sizes given by

\displaystyle|X|=3k+2\quad\text{and}\quad|Y_{1}|=n-|X|.

The edge set of
E_{4}(n,k)
is defined as

\displaystyle E_{4}(n,k)=\binom{X}{2}\cup K[X,Y_{1}].

•

The vertex set of
E_{5}(n,k)
consists of seven parts
X,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{6}
, with sizes satisfying

\displaystyle|X|=12k-2n+9-j,\quad|Y_{1}|+|Y_{2}|+|Y_{3}|=4k+3-|X|,\quad

\displaystyle|Y_{4}|+|Y_{5}|+|Y_{6}|=n-4k-3,\quad|Y_{1}|+|Y_{6}|=n-4k-3+\left\lfloor\frac{j}{3}\right\rfloor,

\displaystyle|Y_{2}|+|Y_{4}|=n-4k-3+\left\lfloor\frac{j+1}{3}\right\rfloor,\quad\text{and}\quad|Y_{3}|+|Y_{5}|=n-4k-3+\left\lfloor\frac{j+2}{3}\right\rfloor,

where
j\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.
The edge set of
E_{5}(n,k)
is defined as

\displaystyle E_{5}(n,k)=\binom{X}{2}\cup K[X,Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}\cup Y_{3}]\cup K[Y_{1}\cup Y_{6},Y_{2}\cup Y_{4},Y_{3}\cup Y_{5}].

Note that
E_{5}(n,k)
is defined only for
k\geq\frac{2n-9}{12}
.

Simple calculations show that

\displaystyle|E_{1}(n,k)|

\displaystyle=\binom{k}{2}+k(n-k)+\left\lfloor\frac{(n-k)^{2}}{3}\right\rfloor\approx\frac{n^{2}}{3}+\frac{kn}{3}-\frac{k^{2}}{6},

\displaystyle|E_{2}(n,k)|

\displaystyle=\binom{2k+1}{2}+\left\lfloor\frac{n^{2}}{3}\right\rfloor\approx\frac{n^{2}}{3}+2k^{2},

\displaystyle|E_{3}(n,k)|

\displaystyle=\binom{2k+1}{2}+(2k+1)(n-2k-1)+\left\lfloor\frac{(n-2k-1)^{2}}{4}\right\rfloor\approx\frac{n^{2}}{4}+kn-k^{2},

\displaystyle|E_{4}(n,k)|

\displaystyle=\binom{3k+2}{2}+(3k+2)(n-3k-2)\approx 3kn-\frac{9k^{2}}{2},

\displaystyle|E_{5}(n,k)|

\displaystyle=\binom{12k-2n+9}{2}+(12k-2n+9)(2n-8k-6)+3\left(n-4k-3\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\approx n^{2}-8kn+24k^{2}.

We conjecture that
E_{1}(n,k),\ldots,E_{5}(n,k)
are the extremal constructions for
\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_{4})
when
n
is large. The following table outlines the extremal range for each class of constructions.

Construction

Extremal Range

E_{1}(n,k)

I_{1}(n)\coloneqq\left[0,\frac{2n-9}{13}\right]

E_{2}(n,k)

I_{2}(n)\coloneqq\left[\frac{2n-9}{13},\frac{n-1}{6}\right]

E_{3}(n,k)

I_{3}(n)\coloneqq\left[\frac{n-1}{6},\frac{4n-11-\sqrt{2n^{2}-4n-5}}{14}\right]

E_{4}(n,k)

I_{4}(n)\coloneqq\left[\frac{4n-11-\sqrt{2n^{2}-4n-5}}{14},\frac{22n-75+\sqrt{28n^{2}-108n+153}}{114}\right]

E_{5}(n,k)

I_{5}(n)\coloneqq\left[\frac{22n-75+\sqrt{28n^{2}-108n+153}}{114},\frac{n-3}{4}\right]

Table 1:
Extremal range for each class of constructions.

1.3
Setup

In this subsection, we present the notations and assumptions that will be used throughout the remainder of this paper, unless otherwise stated.

Let
n\geq 4k\geq 0
be integers and assume that
n
is sufficiently large.
Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph with
\nu(K_{4},G)=k
.
A
4
-tuple of families
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is a
rank-
4
-packing
of
G
if

(i)

\mathcal{A}
is a
K_{4}
-tiling in
G
,

(ii)

\mathcal{B}
is a
K_{3}
-tiling in
G
,

(iii)

\mathcal{C}
is a
K_{2}
-tiling in
G
,

(iv)

\mathcal{D}
is a
K_{1}
-tiling in
G
(i.e. a collection of vertices), and

(v)

V(\mathcal{A})\cup V(\mathcal{B})\cup V(\mathcal{C})\cup V(\mathcal{D})=V(G)
is a partition of
V(G)
.

As suggested in
[
ABHP15
]
, for the remainder of the paper, we additionally assume that
(|\mathcal{A}|,|\mathcal{B}|,|\mathcal{C}|,|\mathcal{D}|)
is
maximized in lexicographic order
among all rank-
4
-packings of
G
.
Note that, based on the assumption on
G
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{A}|=\nu(K_{4},G)=k\quad\text{and}\quad 4|\mathcal{A}|+3|\mathcal{B}|+2|\mathcal{C}|+|\mathcal{D}|=|V(G)|=n.

(1)

Below, we partition
\mathcal{A}
further into six subfamilies.

Let
A,B,C,D\subseteq V(G)
be four disjoint sets such that
G[A]\cong K_{4}
,
G[B]\cong K_{3}
,
G[C]\cong K_{2}
, and
G[D]\cong K_{1}
. We say

•

B

3
-sees

A
if there exists a vertex
v\in A
such that
G[B\cup\{v\}]\cong K_{4}
,

•

B

2
-sees

A
if there exist two vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in A
and two vertices
u_{1},u_{2}\in B
such that
G[\{v_{1},v_{2},u_{1},u_{2}\}]\cong K_{4}
,

•

C

sees

A
if there exist two vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in A
such that
G[C\cup\{v_{1},v_{2}\}]\cong K_{4}
,

•

D

sees

A
if there exist three vertices
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\in A
such that
G[D\cup\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}]\cong K_{4}
,

K_{4}
in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
K_{4}
in
\mathcal{A}_{2,1}
,
\mathcal{A}_{2,2}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{2,3}
K_{4}
in
\mathcal{A}_{3,1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{3,2}
K_{4}
in
\mathcal{A}_{4,1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{4,2}

Figure 3:
Members in
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{4}
.

The family
\mathcal{A}
is partitioned into six subfamilies,
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{6}
, as follows (see Figure
3
)
\colon

•

\mathcal{A}_{1}
consists of members in
\mathcal{A}
that are
3
-seen by at least two members in
\mathcal{B}
,

•

\mathcal{A}_{2}
is the union of three families
\mathcal{A}_{2,1}
,
\mathcal{A}_{2,2}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{2,3}
, where

–

\mathcal{A}_{2,1}
consists of members in
\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{1}
that are
3
-seen by one member in
\mathcal{B}
and
2
-seen by at least one other member in
\mathcal{B}
,

–

\mathcal{A}_{2,2}
consists of members in
\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{1}
that are

-seen by one member in
\mathcal{B}
and seen by at least one copy of
K_{2}

\mathcal{C}
,

–

\mathcal{A}_{2,3}
consists of members in
\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{1}

\mathcal{C}
,

•

\mathcal{A}_{3}
is the union of two families
\mathcal{A}_{3,1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{3,2}
, where

–

\mathcal{A}_{3,1}

\mathcal{A}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2})
that are
2
-seen by at least three members in
\mathcal{B}
,

–

\mathcal{A}_{3,2}
consists of members in
\mathcal{A}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2})
that are
2

\mathcal{B}
and seen by at least one member in
\mathcal{C}
,

•

is the union of two families
\mathcal{A}_{4,1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{4,2}
, where

–

\mathcal{A}_{4,1}
consists of members in
\mathcal{A}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{3})

•

\mathcal{A}_{5}
and
\mathcal{A}_{6}
are obtained through the following process
\colon

Initially, we set
\mathcal{A}_{5}\coloneqq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{A}_{6}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{3}\cup\mathcal{A}_{4})
. If there exists a member, say
Q
, in
\mathcal{A}_{6}
that sends at most
15\left(|\mathcal{A}_{6}|-1\right)
edges to other members in
\mathcal{A}_{6}
, then we update
\mathcal{A}_{5}
and
\mathcal{A}_{6}
by letting
\mathcal{A}_{5}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}_{5}\cup\{Q\}
and
\mathcal{A}_{6}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}_{6}\setminus\{Q\}
. This process is repeated until every member in
\mathcal{A}_{6}
sends more than
15\left(|\mathcal{A}|-1\right)
edges to other members in
\mathcal{A}_{6}
.

Define

\displaystyle(a_{1},\ldots,a_{6},a,b,c,d)\coloneqq\left(|\mathcal{A}_{1}|,\ldots,|\mathcal{A}_{6}|,|\mathcal{A}|,|\mathcal{B}|,|\mathcal{C}|,|\mathcal{D}|\right).

) that

\displaystyle a_{1}+\cdots+a_{6}=a=k\quad\text{and}\quad 4a+3b+2c+d=n.

Outline of the proof
:
We begin by recalling the proof outline of Allen–Böttcher–Hladký–Piguet
[
ABHP14
]
for Theorem
1.2
.
Their approach starts with taking a lexicographically maximized rank-
3
-packing
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{M},\mathcal{J})
of
G
, where
\mathcal{T}
is a
K_{3}
-tiling,
\mathcal{M}
is
K_{2}
-tiling, and
\mathcal{J}
is a
K_{1}
-tiling. The family
\mathcal{T}
is further partitioned into four subfamilies
\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{3},\mathcal{T}_{4}
, based on the connections of its members to those in
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{J}
, and other members in
\mathcal{T}
.
Using the maximality of
(|\mathcal{T}|,|\mathcal{M}|,|\mathcal{J}|)
and clever combinatorial arguments, they derived an upper bound
f(|\mathcal{T}_{1}|,\ldots,|\mathcal{T}_{4}|,|\mathcal{M}|,|\mathcal{J}|)
for the number of edges in terms of six variables
(|\mathcal{T}_{1}|,\ldots,|\mathcal{T}_{4}|,|\mathcal{M}|,|\mathcal{J}|)
.
Finally, they proved that the maximum value of
f(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4},m,j)
over the region

\displaystyle\left\{(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4},m,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{6}\colon t_{1}+t_{2}+t_{3}+t_{4}=k~\text{and}~4k+2m+j=n\right\}.

is given by the number of edges of members in the four classes of extremal constructions.

Our proof builds upon the framework developed by Allen–Böttcher–Hladký–Piguet. However, as they noted “there are some additional difficulties for
r\geq 4
that do not appear for
r=3
”. Indeed, our initial attempt to naively replicate their argument can only yield the desired upper bound (i.e.
\Xi(n,k)
, as defined in Theorem
1.3
) on
\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_{4})
for
k\in\left[0,\frac{n}{8}\right]
. This limitation arises because the maximum value of the upper bound we obtained for
|G|
(derived from the combination of bounds in Section
3
and Lemma
6.2
) agree with
\Xi(n,k)
only for
k\in\left[0,\frac{n}{8}\right]
.

To address this issue, we introduce several key innovations:
We employ the more delicate partition
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{A}_{6}
as defined earlier, where, notably (and somewhat unexpectedly), members of
\mathcal{A}_{3}
do not appear in any extremal construction, yet their inclusion is essential for achieving the desired upper bound.
Significant effort is also required to establish useful upper bounds for the number of edges in
\mathcal{A}_{3}
(see Section
4
),
\mathcal{A}_{4}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}
(see Section
5
), and
\mathcal{A}_{6}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D}
(see Section
6
), respectively.
In fact, we derive multiple upper bounds for these parts, each tailored to specific cases.
Similarly, we employ several distinct
9
-variable functions to upper bound
|G|
(see Section
7
), allowing us to achieve the desired final bound in different cases.
Additionally, to make our calculations less complicated, we ignore the linear terms in the upper bounds we derived and focus only on the quadratic terms. This simplification is one of the reasons for the
O(n)
error term in Theorem
1.3
.

Our approach for the case
r=4
suggests that, to determine
\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_{r})
for general
r\geq 5
, a carefully designed partition of the
K_{r}
-tiling is essential.

Organization of the paper
:
We present some preliminary results in the next section.
In Section
3
, we provide basic upper bounds for the number of edges crossing the parts
\{\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{6},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\}
and inside each part.
In Section
4
, we present improved upper bounds for
e(\mathcal{A}_{3})
.
In Section
5
, we present improved upper bounds for
e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C})
.
In Section
6
, we present improved upper bounds for
e(\mathcal{A}_{6}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})
.
In Section
7
, we combine these upper bounds to prove Theorem
1.4
, after solving certain quadratic (or convex) optimization problems (Sections
7.2
,
7.3
, and
7.4
).
Section
8
contains an application of Theorem
1.3
and a conjecture for general
r\geq 5
.

2
Preliminaries

For every vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
, let
G[S]
denote the
induced subgraph
of
G
on
S
. The number of edges in
G[S]
is denoted by
e_{G}(S)
for simplicity.
We use
G-S
to denote the induced subgraph of
G
on
V(G)\setminus S
.
Given another vertex
T\subseteq V(G)
that is disjoint from
S
, let
G[S,T]
denote the
induced bipartite subgraph
of
G
on
S
and
T
, i.e.
G[S,T]
consists of all edges in
G
that have nonempty intersection with both
S
and
T
. The number of edges in
G[S,T]
is denoted by
e_{G}(S,T)
for simplicity.
Let
\overline{G}
denote the
complement
of
G
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{1}
be an
F_{1}
-tiling in
G
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
be an
F_{2}
-tiling in
G
, and assume that
V(\mathcal{F}_{1})\cap V(\mathcal{F}_{2})=\emptyset
.
We use
e_{G}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
to denote the number of edges in the induced bipartite subgraph
G[V(\mathcal{F}_{1}),V(\mathcal{F}_{2})]
.
In particular, if
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are vertex-disjoint subgraphs of
G
, then
e_{G}(F_{1},F_{2})
is the number of edges in the induced bipartite subgraph
G[V(F_{1}),V(F_{2})]
.
Similarly, we use
e_{G}(\mathcal{F}_{1})
and
e_{G}(F_{1})
to denote the number of edges in the induced subgraphs
G[V(\mathcal{F}_{1})]
and
G[V(F_{1})]
, respectively.
We will omit the subscript
G
from the notations defined above if it is clear from the context.

The following classical theorems by Erdős–Gallai will be useful for us.

Theorem 2.1

(Erdős–Gallai
[
EG59
]
)
.

Let
n\geq t\geq 1
be integers satisfying
n\geq 2t
.
Suppose that
G
is a graph on
n
vertices with
\nu(G)\leq t
.
Then

\displaystyle|G|\leq\max\left\{t(n-t)+\binom{t}{2},~\binom{2t+1}{2}\right\}\leq tn.

For every integer
t\geq 1
, let
P_{t}
denote the path on
t
vertices.

Theorem 2.2

(Erdős–Gallai
[
EG59
]
)
.

Let
n\geq t\geq 2
be integers. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{ex}(n,P_{t})\leq\frac{(t-2)n}{2}.

For every integer
t\geq 1
, let
L_{t}
denote the graph obtained from the complete graph
K_{t}
by removing a Hamiltonian cycle.
Observe that
L_{t}
is contained in
K_{\lceil t/2\rceil}[2,2,\ldots,2]
(the complete
\lceil t/2\rceil
-partite graph with ecah part has exactly two vertices), and hence, the chromatic number
\chi(L_{t})
of
L_{t}
satisfies

\displaystyle\chi(L_{t})=t-\lfloor t/2\rfloor=\lceil t/2\rceil.

Given two graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, the
joint

G_{1}\times G_{2}
is the graph obtained from the vertex-disjoint union of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
) by adding all edges that have nonempty intersection with both
V(G_{1})
and
V(G_{2})
, i.e.

\displaystyle G_{1}\times G_{2}\coloneqq G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup\left\{\{u,v\}\colon(u,v)\in V(G_{1})\times V(G_{2})\right\}.

An additional observation is that, for
t\geq 4
, the graph
L_{t}
is contained in
P_{4}\times K_{\lceil t/2\rceil-2}[2,2,\ldots,2]
.
So by a classical theorem of Simonovits
[
Sim74
, Theorem 1]
(see also
[
MQ20
, Theorem 1.2]
), we derive the following upper bound for
\mathrm{ex}(n,L_{t})
.

Theorem 2.3

(Simonovits
[
Sim74
]
)
.

Let
t\geq 5
be an integer. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{ex}(n,L_{t})=\left(1-\frac{1}{\lceil t/2\rceil-1}\right)\frac{n^{2}}{2}+O(n).

The following property of
L_{t}
will be used in Section
5
.

Fact 2.4
.

Let
t\geq 5
be an integer. The induced subgraph of
L_{t}
on every subset of size
4
contains a copy of
P_{4}
.

Given integers
m,n\geq 0
, the
bipartite Turán number

\mathrm{ex}(m,n,F)
of a graph
F
is defined as the maximum number of edges in an
F
-free
m
by
n
bipartite graph.
We will use a theorem by Gyárfás–Rousseau–Schelp
[
GRS84
]
on
\mathrm{ex}(m,n,P_{t})
. Below, we present a weaker version of their result.

Theorem 2.5

(Gyárfás–Rousseau–Schelp
[
GRS84
]
)
.

Let
n,m\geq 1
and
t\geq 3
be integers.
Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{ex}(m,n,P_{t})\leq\frac{t-2}{2}(m+n).

The
Zarankiewicz number

Z(m,n,s,t)
is the maximum number of edges in an
m
by
n
bipartite graph
G
such that
G
contains no copy of the complete bipartite graph
K_{s,t}
, where the
s
-vertex part of
K_{s,t}
is contained in the
m
-vertex part of
G
and the
t
-vertex part of
K_{s,t}
is contained in the
n
-vertex part of
G
.
We will use the following upper bound on
Z(m,n,a,b)
by Čulík
[
Č56
]
(see also
[
FS13
, Theorem 3.28]
).

Theorem 2.6

(Čulík
[
Č56
]
)
.

Suppose that
n,m\geq 0
are integers satisfying
n\geq(b-1)\binom{m}{a}
.
Then

\displaystyle Z(m,n,a,b)=(a-1)n+(b-1)\binom{m}{a}.

The following simple facts are useful for finding edges with certain properties between members of
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D}
.

Fact 2.7
.

Let
H=H[V_{1},V_{2}]
be a bipartite graph.

(i)

Suppose that
(|V_{1}|,|V_{2}|)=(2,3)
and
|H|\geq 5
. Then there exists a vertex in
V_{1}
that is adjacent to all vertices in
V_{2}
. There also exists a pair of vertices in
V_{2}
such that both vertices in
V_{1}
are adjacent to this pair.

(ii)

Suppose that
(|V_{1}|,|V_{2}|)=(2,4)
and
|H|\geq 5
. Then there exists a vertex in
V_{1}
that is adjacent to at least three vertices in
V_{2}
.

(iii)

Suppose that
(|V_{1}|,|V_{2}|)=(2,4)
and
|H|\geq 6
. Then there exists a pair of vertices in
V_{2}
that are adjacent to both vertices in
V_{1}
.

(iv)

Suppose that
(|V_{1}|,|V_{2}|)=(3,3)
and
|H|\geq 7
. Then there exists a vertex in
V_{1}
that is adjacent to all vertices in
V_{2}
.

(v)

Suppose that
(|V_{1}|,|V_{2}|)=(4,3)
and
|H|\geq 9
. Then there exists a vertex in
V_{1}
that is adjacent to all vertices in
V_{2}
.

(vi)

Suppose that
(|V_{1}|,|V_{2}|)=(4,3)
and
|H|\geq 8
. Then there exist
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}\subseteq V_{1}
and
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\subseteq V_{2}
such that
\{u_{i},v_{j}\}\in H
for every
(i,j)\in[2]\times[2]
.

(vii)

Suppose that
(|V_{1}|,|V_{2}|)=(4,3)
and
|H|\geq 7
. Then there exists a vertex in
V_{2}
that is adjacent to at least three vertices in
V_{1}
.

3
Local estimation I: basic upper bounds

In this subsection, we establish basic upper bounds for the number of edges crossing the parts in
\{\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{6},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\}
and for the edges within each part.

The following simple fact follows directly from the definitions of
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
and the maximality of
(|\mathcal{A}|,|\mathcal{B}|,|\mathcal{C}|,|\mathcal{D}|)
.

Fact 3.1
.

The following statements hold.

(i)

e(\mathcal{B})\leq 3b+6\binom{b}{2}=3b^{2}
,

(ii)

e(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})\leq 4bc
,

(iii)

e(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{D})\leq 2bd
,

(iv)

e(\mathcal{C})\leq c^{2}
,

(v)

e(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\leq cd
,

(vi)

e(\mathcal{D})=0
.

Consequently,
e(\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})\leq\Psi(b,c,d)
, where

\displaystyle\Psi(b,c,d)\coloneqq 3b^{2}+4bc+2bd+c^{2}+cd.

(2)

3.1
Upper bounds for
e(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{A}_{j})

In this subsection, we establish an upper bound for
e(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{A}_{j})
for each
(i,j)\in[6]\times[6]
. For convenience, we set
e(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i})\coloneqq e(\mathcal{A}_{i})
for every
i\in[6]
.

The following lemma provides an upper bound for
e(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{i})
for every
i\in[6]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

The following statements hold for every member
Q=\{p,q,s,t\}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

(i)

Suppose that
i\in[4]
. Then
e(Q,Q_{i})\leq 15
.

(ii)

Let
i\in[6]
and
Q_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Suppose that
e(Q,Q_{i})\geq 14
. Then
Q_{i}
cannot be
3
-seen by any member of
\mathcal{B}
. In particular,
e(Q,Q_{1})\leq 13
for every
Q_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\setminus\{Q\}
, and hence,

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{1})\leq 13\binom{a_{1}}{2}+6a_{1}\leq\frac{13a_{1}^{2}}{2}.

(iii)

Let
i\in[2,6]
and
Q_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Suppose that
e(Q,Q_{i})\geq 15
. Then
Q_{i}
cannot be
2
-seen by any member of
\mathcal{B}
and cannot be seen by any member of
\mathcal{C}
.

(iv)

Let
i\in[2,6]
and
Q_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. If
|\mathcal{A}_{1}|\geq 2
, then
e(\mathcal{A}_{1},Q_{i})\leq 13a_{1}
. Consequently, for every
i\in[2,6]
,

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq 13a_{1}a_{i}+3a_{i}.

.

, contradicting the maximality of

.

(iv)
. Fix

and

The following lemma provides an upper bound for
e(\mathcal{A}_{5}\cup\mathcal{A}_{6})
.

Lemma 3.6
.

We have

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{5}\cup\mathcal{A}_{6})\leq 15\binom{a_{5}}{2}+6a_{5}+15a_{5}a_{6}+16\binom{a_{6}}{2}+6a_{6}\leq\frac{15a_{5}^{2}}{2}+15a_{5}a_{6}+8a_{6}^{2}.

Proof of Lemma
3.6
.

We have the trivial upper bound
e(\mathcal{A}_{6})\leq 16\binom{a_{6}}{2}+6a_{6}
, so it suffices to show that

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{5})+e(\mathcal{A}_{5},\mathcal{A}_{6})\leq 15\binom{a_{5}}{2}+6a_{5}+15a_{5}a_{6}.

Recall from the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{5}
that there exists an ordering
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{a_{5}}
of elements in
\mathcal{A}_{5}
such that for every
i\in[a_{5}]
,

\displaystyle e(Q_{i},Q_{i+1}\cup\cdots\cup Q_{a_{5}}\cup V(\mathcal{A}_{6}))\leq 15\left(a_{5}-i+|\mathcal{A}_{6}|\right)=15(a_{5}+a_{6}-i).

It follows that

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{5})+e(\mathcal{A}_{5},\mathcal{A}_{6})

\displaystyle\leq 6a_{5}+\sum_{i=1}^{a_{5}}e(Q_{i},Q_{i+1}\cup\cdots\cup Q_{a_{5}}\cup V(\mathcal{A}_{6}))

\displaystyle\leq 6a_{5}+\sum_{i=1}^{a_{5}}15(a_{5}+a_{6}-i)=6a_{5}+15\binom{a_{5}}{2}+15a_{5}a_{6},

(3)

as desired.

3.2

e(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})

for each

.

The following lemma provides an upper bound for
e(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})

Lemma 3.7
.

The following statements hold for every
Q=\{p,q,s,t\}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

(i)

e(Q,B)\leq 9
for every
B\in\mathcal{B}
.

(ii)

e(Q,C)\leq 6
for every
C\in\mathcal{C}
.

(iii)

e(Q,D)\leq 3
for every
D\in\mathcal{D}
.

Consequently,

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})\leq(9b+6c+3d)a_{1}.

increases by one.

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, there is at most one member, say
B^{\prime}
, in
\mathcal{B}
that can
3
-see
Q
; otherwise,
Q
would belong to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. It follows that
e(Q,B)\leq 4\cdot 2=8
for every
B\in\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B^{\prime}\}
. This proves Lemma
3.8

(i)
. In particular, it follows that

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{B})=\sum_{Q_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}}e(Q_{2},\mathcal{B})\leq a_{2}\left(8(|\mathcal{B}|-1)+3\cdot 4\right)=8a_{2}b+4a_{2}.

Next, we prove Lemma
3.8

(ii)
. The proof for the case where
Q\in\mathcal{A}_{2,1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2,2}
is similar to that of Lemma
3.7

(ii)
and is therefore omitted.
So it suffices to consider the case
Q\in\mathcal{A}_{2,3}
.
Fix
C\in\mathcal{C}
. Suppose to the contrary that
e(Q,C)\geq 7=8-1
.
Then there exist three vertices in
Q
that are all adjacent to both vertices in

. By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{2,3}
, there exists a member

such that both vertices of

are adjacent to the same pair of vertices in
Q
. However, the rotation shown in Figure

would increase the value of
|\mathcal{B}|
(while keeping
|\mathcal{A}|
unchanged), contradicting the maximality of
|\mathcal{B}|
.

The proof for Lemma
3.8

(iii)
is similar (see Figure

), so we omit it here.

The following lemma provides an upper bound for
e(\mathcal{A}_{3},\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})

Lemma 3.9
.

The following statements hold for every

.

(i)

e(Q,B)\leq 8
for every

for all but at most one

for all but at most one

.

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{3},\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})\leq(8b+5c+3d)a_{3}+4a_{3}.

4
Local estimation II
\colon

e(\mathcal{A}_{3})

In this section, we prove the following improved upper bound for
e(\mathcal{A}_{3})
, which refines the bound
e(\mathcal{A}_{3})\leq\frac{15a_{3}^{2}}{2}
given in Lemma
3.4

(i)
.

Lemma 4.1
.

We have

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{3})\leq 7a_{3}^{2}.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

Let us define an auxiliary graph
H
whose vertex set is
\mathcal{A}_{3}
, and two members
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{3}
are adjacent in
H
iff
e(Q,Q^{\prime})=15
(recall from Lemma
3.4

(i)
that
e(Q_{3},Q_{3}^{\prime})\leq 15
for every pair
Q_{3},Q_{3}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{3}
).
For convenience, for every vertex
v\in V(H)
, we use
Q_{v}
to denote the corresponding member in
\mathcal{A}_{3}
.

Let
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{M},\mathcal{I})
be a rank-
3
-packing of
H
, defined as follows
\colon

•

\mathcal{T}
is a
K_{3}
-tiling in
H
,

•

\mathcal{M}
is a
K_{2}
-tiling in
H
,

•

\mathcal{I}
is a
K_{1}
-tiling in
H
, and

•

V(\mathcal{T})\cup V(\mathcal{M})\cup V(\mathcal{I})=V(H)
is a partition of
V(H)
,

with the condition that
(|\mathcal{T}|,|\mathcal{M}|,|\mathcal{I}|)
is maximum in the lexicographical order. Note that

\displaystyle 3|\mathcal{T}|+2|\mathcal{M}|+|\mathcal{I}|=|V(H)|=|\mathcal{A}_{3}|=a_{3}.

(4)

Let

\displaystyle U_{1}\coloneqq\bigcup_{u\in\mathcal{T}}V(Q_{u}),\quad U_{2}\coloneqq\bigcup_{u\in\mathcal{M}}V(Q_{u}),\quad\text{and}\quad U_{3}\coloneqq\bigcup_{u\in\mathcal{I}}V(Q_{u}).

Note that

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{3})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[3]}e_{G}(U_{i})+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}e_{G}(U_{i},U_{j}),

(5)

and note from the definition of
H
that

\displaystyle e_{G}(U_{3})\leq 14\binom{|\mathcal{I}|}{2}+\binom{4}{2}\cdot|\mathcal{I}|=7|\mathcal{I}|^{2}-|\mathcal{I}|.

(6)

Q_{u_{2}}
Q_{u_{3}}
Q_{v_{2}}

Claim 4.2
.

Let
T=\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}\in\mathcal{T}
and
M=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in\mathcal{M}
. For every
(i,j)\in[3]\times[2]
, we have

\displaystyle e_{G}(Q_{u_{i}},Q_{v_{j}})\leq 12.

Consequently,

\displaystyle e_{G}(U_{1},U_{2})\leq 12\cdot 3|\mathcal{T}|\cdot 2|\mathcal{M}|=72|\mathcal{T}||\mathcal{M}|.

Proof of Claim
4.2
.

Fix
T=\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}\in\mathcal{T}
and
M=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in\mathcal{M}
. Assume that
Q_{u_{i}}=\{p_{i},q_{i},s_{i},t_{i}\}
for
i\in[3]
and
Q_{v_{i}}=\{p_{i}^{\prime},q_{i}^{\prime},s_{i}^{\prime},t_{i}^{\prime}\}
for
i\in[2]
. Suppose to the contrary that this claim is not true. By symmetry, we may assume that
e_{G}(Q_{u_{3}},Q_{v_{1}})\geq 13
.

By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{3}
(see Figure
3
), there exist three pairwise disjoint edges (which belong to members of
\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}
)
\{x_{1},y_{1}\},\{x_{2},y_{2}\},\{x_{2}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime}\}\in G-V(\mathcal{A})
such that

•

both vertices in
\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
are adjacent to a certain pair of vertices in
Q_{u_{1}}
,

•

both vertices in
\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
are adjacent to a certain pair of vertices in
Q_{u_{2}}
,

•

and both vertices in
\{x_{2}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime}\}
are adjacent to a certain pair of vertices in
Q_{v_{2}}
.

By symmetry, we may assume that
\{p_{1},q_{1}\}\subseteq Q_{u_{1}}
,
\{p_{2},q_{2}\}\subseteq Q_{u_{2}}
, and
\{p_{2}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime}\}\subseteq Q_{v_{2}}
are such vertices.

Since
e_{G}(Q_{u_{3}},Q_{v_{1}})\geq 13=16-3
, there exists a vertex in
Q_{v_{1}}
that is adjacent to all four vertices in
Q_{v_{3}}
, and by symmetry, we may assume that
p_{1}^{\prime}
is a such vertex in
Q_{v_{1}}
. Notice that

\displaystyle|G[\{s_{2}^{\prime},t_{2}^{\prime}\},\{q_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime}\}]|\geq 15-(4+4+2)=5.

By Fact
2.7

(i)
, there exists a pair of vertices in
\{q_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime}\}
that are adjacent to both vertices in
\{s_{2}^{\prime},t_{2}^{\prime}\}
.

Note that

\displaystyle|G[\{s_{1},t_{1}\},\{s_{2},t_{2}\}]|\geq 15-(4+4+2+2)=3.

So there exists a vertex in
\{s_{1},t_{1}\}
that is adjacent to both vertices in
\{s_{2},t_{2}\}
, and by symmetry, we may assume that
s_{1}
is such a vertex.

Since
e(Q_{u_{1}},Q_{u_{3}})\geq 15=16-1
and
e(Q_{u_{2}},Q_{u_{3}})\geq 15=16-1
, we have

\displaystyle|G[\{s_{1},s_{2},t_{2}\},Q_{u_{3}}]|\geq 3\cdot 4-(1+1)=10.

It follows that there exists a vertex in
Q_{u_{3}}
that is adjacent to all vertices in
\{s_{1},s_{2},t_{2}\}
. Recall that the vertex
p_{1}^{\prime}\in Q_{v_{1}}
is adjacent to all four vertices in
Q_{u_{3}}
.
However, the rotation shown in Figure
25
would increase the value of
|\mathcal{A}|
, contradicting the maximality of
(|\mathcal{A}|,|\mathcal{B}|,|\mathcal{C}|,|\mathcal{D}|)
.

.

T=\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\},T^{\prime}=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}\in\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle e_{G}(U_{1})\leq 12\cdot 3\cdot 3\cdot\binom{|\mathcal{T}|}{2}+\binom{4}{2}\cdot 3|\mathcal{T}|+3\cdot 15\cdot|\mathcal{T}|=54|\mathcal{T}|^{2}+9|\mathcal{T}|.

Claim 4.5
.

M=\{u_{1},u_{2}\},M^{\prime}=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle e_{G}\left(Q_{u_{1}}\cup Q_{u_{2}},Q_{v_{1}}\cup Q_{v_{2}}\right)\leq 56.

(7)

Consequently, if
|\mathcal{M}|\geq 2
, then

\displaystyle e_{G}(U_{2})\leq 56\cdot\binom{|\mathcal{M}|}{2}+\binom{4}{2}\cdot 2|\mathcal{M}|+15\cdot|\mathcal{M}|=28|\mathcal{M}|^{2}-|\mathcal{M}|.

Proof of Claim
4.5
.

Fix
M=\{u_{1},u_{2}\},M^{\prime}=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in\mathcal{M}
. Assume that
Q_{u_{i}}=\{p_{i},q_{i},s_{i},t_{i}\}
and

for

. Suppose to the contrary that (
7
) fails.
Then by the Pigeonhole Principle, there exists a pair
(i,j)\in[2]\times[2]
such that
e(Q_{u_{i}},Q_{v_{j}})\geq\lceil 57/4\rceil=15
. By symmetry, we may assume that
(i,j)=(2,1)
.

It follows from the maximality of
(|\mathcal{T}|,|\mathcal{M}|,|\mathcal{I}|)
(in particular, the maximality of
|\mathcal{T}|
) that

•

e(Q_{u_{1}},Q_{v_{1}})\leq 14
, since otherwise,
\{u_{1},u_{2},v_{1}\}
would be in
\mathcal{T}
,

•

and
e(Q_{u_{2}},Q_{v_{2}})\leq 14
, since otherwise,
\{v_{1},v_{2},u_{2}\}
would be in
\mathcal{T}
.

Combining this with the assumption (
7
), we obtain

\displaystyle e_{G}(Q_{u_{1}},Q_{v_{2}})

\displaystyle=e_{G}\left(Q_{u_{1}}\cup Q_{u_{2}},Q_{v_{1}}\cup Q_{v_{2}}\right)-e_{G}(Q_{u_{1}},Q_{v_{1}})-e_{G}(Q_{u_{2}},Q_{v_{1}})-e_{G}(Q_{u_{2}},Q_{v_{2}})

\displaystyle\geq 57-14-15-14=14.

Similar to the proof of Claim
4.2
, by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{3}
, there exist three pairwise disjoint edges (which belong to members in
\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}
)
\{x_{1},y_{1}\},\{x_{2},y_{2}\},\{x_{2}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime}\}\in G-V(\mathcal{A})
such that

•

both vertices in
\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
are adjacent to a certain pair of vertices in
Q_{u_{1}}
,

•

both vertices in
\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
are adjacent to a certain pair of vertices in
Q_{u_{2}}
,

•

and both vertices in
\{x_{2}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime}\}
are adjacent to a certain pair of vertices in
Q_{v_{2}}
.

By symmetry, we may assume that
\{p_{1},q_{1}\}\subseteq Q_{u_{1}}
,
\{p_{2},q_{2}\}\subseteq Q_{u_{2}}
, and
\{p_{2}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime}\}\subseteq Q_{v_{2}}
are such vertices. Also, similar to the proof of Claim
4.2
, we may assume that
s_{1}
is adjacent to both vertices in
\{s_{2},t_{2}\}
.

Since
|G[Q_{v_{1}},Q_{u_{1}}]|\geq 14=16-2
, there exist two vertices in
Q_{v_{1}}
that are adjacent to
s_{1}
, and by symmetry, we may assume that
\{p_{1}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime}\}
are such two vertices. Since

, one of the vertices in

must be adjacent to both
\{s_{2},t_{2}\}
, and by symmetry, we may assume that
p_{1}^{\prime}

\{s_{2},t_{2}\}
. Note that

\displaystyle|G[\{q_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime}\},\{s_{2}^{\prime},t_{2}^{\prime}\}]|\geq 15-(4+2\cdot 3)=5.

So it follows from Fact
2.7

(i)
that both vertices in

However, the rotation shown in Figure

would increase the size of

, contradicting the maximality of
(|\mathcal{A}|,|\mathcal{B}|,|\mathcal{C}|,|\mathcal{D}|)

Claim 4.6
.

For every
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}\in\mathcal{M}
and
w\in\mathcal{I}
, we have

\displaystyle e(Q_{u_{1}}\cup Q_{u_{2}},Q_{w})\leq 29.

(8)

Moreover, if equality holds and

, then for every

\displaystyle e(Q_{v_{1}}\cup Q_{v_{2}},Q_{w})\leq 24.

(9)

In particular, for every pair
\{u_{1},u_{2}\},\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(e(Q_{u_{1}}\cup Q_{u_{2}},Q_{w})+e(Q_{v_{1}}\cup Q_{v_{2}},Q_{w})\right)\leq 28.

Consequently,

\displaystyle e(U_{2},U_{3})\leq\begin{cases}29|\mathcal{I}|,&\quad\text{if}\quad|\mathcal{M}|=1,\\[5.0pt]
28|\mathcal{M}||\mathcal{I}|,&\quad\text{if}\quad|\mathcal{M}|\geq 2.\end{cases}

).

) holds with equality and

.

.

Similar to the proof of Claim
4.2
, by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{3}
, there exist three pairwise disjoint edges
\{x_{1},y_{1}\},\{x_{2},y_{2}\},\{x_{2}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime}\}\in G-V(\mathcal{A})

•

both vertices in

,

both vertices in

,

•

and both vertices in
\{x_{2}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime}\}
are adjacent to a certain pair of vertices in
Q_{v_{2}}
.

By symmetry, we may assume that
\{p_{1},q_{1}\}\subseteq Q_{u_{1}}
,
\{p_{2},q_{2}\}\subseteq Q_{u_{2}}
, and
\{p_{2}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime}\}\subseteq Q_{v_{2}}
are such vertices. Also, similar to the proof of Claim
4.2
, we may assume that
s_{1}
is adjacent to both vertices in
\{s_{2},t_{2}\}
.

Since
e(Q_{u_{1}}\cup Q_{u_{2}},Q_{w})=29=32-3
, there exists a vertex in
Q_{w}
that is adjacent to all three vertices in
\{s_{1},s_{2},t_{2}\}
, and by symmetry, we may assume that
p
is such a vertex.
Note that

\displaystyle|G[\{q,s,t\},Q_{v_{1}}]|\geq 13-4=9.

So it follows from Fact
2.7

(v)
that there exists a vertex in
Q_{v_{1}}
that is adjacent to all three vertices in
\{q,s,t\}
, and by symmetry, we may assume that
q_{1}^{\prime}
is such a vertex.
Additionally, since

\displaystyle|G[\{q_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime}\},\{s_{2}^{\prime},t_{2}^{\prime}\}]|\geq 15-(4+4+2)=5,

by Fact
2.7

(i)
,
there exist two vertices in
\{q_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime}\}
that are adjacent to both vertices in
\{s_{2}^{\prime},t_{2}^{\prime}\}
.
However, the rotation shown in Figure
28
would increase the value of
|\mathcal{A}|
, contradicting the maximality of
(|\mathcal{A}|,|\mathcal{B}|,|\mathcal{C}|,|\mathcal{D}|)
.

Suppose that
|\mathcal{M}|\geq 2
.
Then it follows from (
4
), (
5
), (
6
), Claims
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
,
4.5
, and
4.6
that

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{3})

\displaystyle\leq 54|\mathcal{T}|^{2}+9|\mathcal{T}|+28|\mathcal{M}|^{2}-|\mathcal{M}|+72|\mathcal{T}||\mathcal{M}|+42|\mathcal{T}||\mathcal{I}|+28|\mathcal{M}||\mathcal{I}|+7|\mathcal{I}|^{2}-|\mathcal{I}|

\displaystyle\leq 63|\mathcal{T}|^{2}+28|\mathcal{M}|^{2}+84|\mathcal{T}||\mathcal{M}|+42|\mathcal{T}||\mathcal{I}|+28|\mathcal{M}||\mathcal{I}|+7|\mathcal{I}|^{2}

\displaystyle=7(3|\mathcal{T}|+2|\mathcal{M}|+|\mathcal{I}|)^{2}=7a_{3}^{2},

as desired.

Suppose that
|\mathcal{M}|=1
. Then similar to the argument above, and using the trivial equality
29|\mathcal{M}||\mathcal{I}|-|\mathcal{I}|=28|\mathcal{M}||\mathcal{I}|
,
we have

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{3})

\displaystyle\leq 54|\mathcal{T}|^{2}+9|\mathcal{T}|+27|\mathcal{M}|+72|\mathcal{T}||\mathcal{M}|+42|\mathcal{T}||\mathcal{I}|+29|\mathcal{M}||\mathcal{I}|+7|\mathcal{I}|^{2}-|\mathcal{I}|

\displaystyle\leq 63|\mathcal{T}|^{2}+28|\mathcal{M}|^{2}+84|\mathcal{T}||\mathcal{M}|+42|\mathcal{T}||\mathcal{I}|+28|\mathcal{M}||\mathcal{I}|+7|\mathcal{I}|^{2}

\displaystyle=7(3|\mathcal{T}|+2|\mathcal{M}|+|\mathcal{I}|)^{2}=7a_{3}^{2},

also as desired.
This completes the proof of Lemma
4.1
.

5
Local estimation III
\colon

e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C})

In this section, we establish an improved upper for
e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C})
, refining the following bounds given in Lemma
3.5

(i)
and Lemma
3.10

(i)

(ii)
:

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{4})\leq\frac{15a_{4}^{2}}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C})\leq(7b+5c)a_{4}+18a_{4}.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
x,y\geq 0
be real numbers. Define

\displaystyle g_{\alpha}(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}7\alpha x^{2}+7xy,&\quad\text{if}\quad x\leq\frac{y}{2\alpha},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{15\alpha x^{2}}{2}+\frac{13xy}{2}+\frac{y^{2}}{8\alpha},&\quad\text{if}\quad x>\frac{y}{2\alpha},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\quad\text{and}\quad h_{\alpha}(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}7\alpha x^{2}+5xy,&\quad\text{if}\quad x\leq\frac{y}{2\alpha},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{15\alpha x^{2}}{2}+\frac{9xy}{2}+\frac{y^{2}}{8\alpha},&\quad\text{if}\quad x>\frac{y}{2\alpha}.\end{cases}

Additionally, define

\displaystyle\hat{g}(x,y)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{21x^{2}}{4}+7xy,&\quad\text{if}\quad x\leq\frac{6y}{5},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{45x^{2}}{8}+\frac{61xy}{10}+\frac{27y^{2}}{50},&\quad\text{if}\quad x\in\left[\frac{6y}{5},\frac{(86-4\sqrt{210})y}{15}\right],\\[5.0pt]
\frac{28x^{2}}{5}+\frac{479xy}{75}+\frac{103y^{2}}{1125},&\quad\text{if}\quad x\in\left[\frac{(86-4\sqrt{210})y}{15},\frac{56y}{15}\right],\\[5.0pt]
\frac{651x^{2}}{116}+\frac{913xy}{145}+\frac{113y^{2}}{435},&\quad\text{if}\quad x\in\left[\frac{56y}{15},\frac{38y}{5}\right]\\[5.0pt]
\frac{45x^{2}}{8}+\frac{61xy}{10}+\frac{151y^{2}}{150},&\quad\text{if}\quad x\geq\frac{38y}{5}.\end{cases}

Lemma 5.1
.

There exists an absolute constant
C_{\ref{LEMMA:A4BC-together}}>0
such that

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C})\leq C_{\ref{LEMMA:A4BC-together}}n+\begin{cases}g_{1/2}(a_{4},b)+h_{1/2}(a_{4},c),&\quad\text{if}\quad d<\frac{b+c}{2},\\[5.0pt]
\hat{g}(a_{4},b)+h_{1/4}(a_{4},c),&\quad\text{if}\quad d\geq\frac{b+c}{2}.\end{cases}

Lemma
5.1
will follow from Lemmas
5.2
,
5.3
, and
5.9
, which will be proved in the following two subsections.

5.1
\mathcal{A}_{4}
and
\mathcal{B}

In this subsection, we prove the following two upper bounds for
\alpha\cdot e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B})
.

Lemma 5.2
.

For every
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C_{\ref{LEMMA:A4-B-bipartite-bound}}=C_{\ref{LEMMA:A4-B-bipartite-bound}}(\alpha)>0
such that

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B})\leq g_{\alpha}(a_{4},b)+C_{\ref{LEMMA:A4-B-bipartite-bound}}n.

Lemma 5.3
.

There exists an absolute constant
C_{\ref{LEMMA:A4-B-10-partite-bound}}>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}\cdot e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B})\leq\hat{g}(a_{4},b)+C_{\ref{LEMMA:A4-B-10-partite-bound}}n.

Given
Q\in\mathcal{A}
and

, we say

1
-sees

(see Figure

such that

is adjacent to both

and

and

, and

and

.

The following fact follows easily from the definitions (refer to the definitions of

and

either

.

.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
Recall that
b=|\mathcal{B}|
.
Suppose that

. Then it follows from Lemma

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B})\leq\alpha\cdot\frac{15a_{4}^{2}}{2}+3\cdot 4\cdot a_{4}b\leq g_{\alpha}(a_{4},b)+O_{\alpha}(n),

Define an auxiliary graph

and two members

(i)
that

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{4})\leq 15|H|+14\left(\binom{a_{4}}{2}-|H|\right)+6a_{4}=7a_{4}^{2}+|H|-a_{4}.

(10)

Define an auxiliary bipartite graph

and a pair of members

It follows from the definition of

and Lemma

that

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\leq|Y|\cdot\left(7(b-2)+2\cdot 3\cdot 4\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(|\mathcal{A}_{4}|-|Y|\right)\cdot\left(7\cdot\frac{b+7}{2}+6\cdot\left(b-2-\frac{b+7}{2}\right)+2\cdot 3\cdot 4\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{13a_{4}b}{2}+\frac{b|Y|}{2}+\frac{31a_{4}}{2}-\frac{11|Y|}{2}\leq\frac{13a_{4}b}{2}+\frac{b|Y|}{2}+16n.

(11)

Claim 5.6
.

The induced subgraph

,

.

Proof of Claim
5.6
.

Suppose to the contrary that there exist a copy of
P_{4}
, say
Q_{1}Q_{2}Q_{3}Q_{4}
, in

.
For each

, let

that

.
By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{4}
, every member in

is

. Therefore, by Fact

1
-see both

and

It follows from Claim
5.6
that

\displaystyle|H|\leq|Y|+|Y|(a_{4}-|Y|)+\binom{a_{4}-|Y|}{2}=\frac{a_{4}^{2}}{2}-\frac{|Y|^{2}}{2}+\frac{3|Y|}{2}-\frac{a_{4}}{2}.

Combining this with (
10
), we obtain

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{4})\leq 7a_{4}^{2}+\frac{a_{4}^{2}}{2}-\frac{|Y|^{2}}{2}+\frac{3|Y|}{2}-\frac{a_{4}}{2}-a_{4}\leq\frac{15a_{4}^{2}}{2}-\frac{|Y|^{2}}{2}.

Combining this with (
11
), we obtain

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\left(\frac{15a_{4}^{2}}{2}-\frac{|Y|^{2}}{2}\right)+\frac{13a_{4}b}{2}+\frac{b|Y|}{2}+16n

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(|Y|-\frac{b}{2\alpha}\right)^{2}+\frac{b^{2}}{8\alpha}+\frac{15\alpha a_{4}^{2}}{2}+\frac{13a_{4}b}{2}+16n.

Viewing
-\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(|Y|-\frac{b}{2\alpha}\right)^{2}+\frac{b^{2}}{8\alpha}+\frac{15\alpha\cdot a_{4}^{2}}{2}+\frac{13a_{4}b}{2}+16n
as a quadratic polynomial in
|Y|
, and noting that

, we obtain

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}7\alpha a_{4}^{2}+7a_{4}b+16n,&\quad\text{if}\quad a_{4}\leq\frac{b}{2\alpha},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{15\alpha a_{4}^{2}}{2}+\frac{13a_{4}b}{2}+\frac{b^{2}}{8\alpha}+16n,&\quad\text{if}\quad a_{4}>\frac{b}{2\alpha}.\end{cases}

This completes the proof of Lemma

\displaystyle-\biggl(\frac{z_{2}^{2}}{28}+\frac{z_{3}^{2}}{14}+\frac{z_{4}^{2}}{8}+\frac{z_{5}^{2}}{6}+\frac{(z_{6}+\cdots+z_{10})^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\quad+z_{5}z_{7}+(z_{4}+z_{5})z_{8}+(z_{3}+z_{4}+z_{5})z_{9}+(z_{2}+z_{3}+z_{4}+z_{5})z_{10}\bigg).

Combining this with (
10
), we obtain

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{4})\leq\frac{15a_{4}^{2}}{2}+\hat{\eta}(z_{2},\ldots,z_{10})+O(n).

(18)

Next, we consider
e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B})
.
For every
i\in[10]
and
Q\in Z_{i}
, it follows from the definition of
Z_{i}
and Lemma
3.10

(i)
that

\displaystyle e(Q,\mathcal{B})\leq 2\cdot 3\cdot 4+7\left(\frac{i\cdot b}{10}+10^{5}-2\right)+6\left(b-\frac{i\cdot b}{10}-10^{5}\right)=6b+\frac{i\cdot b}{10}+10^{5}+10.

Therefore,

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B})=\sum_{i\in[10]}\sum_{Q\in Z_{i}}e(Q,\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in[10]}z_{i}\left(6b+\frac{i\cdot b}{10}+10^{5}+10\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{61a_{4}b}{10}+\sum_{i\in[2,10]}\frac{(i-1)bz_{i}}{10}+(10^{5}+10)a_{4}.

Combining this with (
18
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}\cdot e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{4}\left(\frac{15a_{4}^{2}}{2}+\hat{\eta}(z_{2},\ldots,z_{10})\right)+\frac{61a_{4}b}{10}+\sum_{i\in[2,10]}\frac{(i-1)bz_{i}}{10}+O(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{45a_{4}^{2}}{8}+\frac{61a_{4}b}{10}+\frac{3}{4}\cdot\hat{\eta}(z_{2},\ldots,z_{10})+\sum_{i\in[2,10]}\frac{(i-1)bz_{i}}{10}+O(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{45a_{4}^{2}}{8}+\frac{61a_{4}b}{10}+\eta(b,z_{2},\ldots,z_{10})+O(n),

where

\displaystyle\eta(b,z_{2},\ldots,z_{10})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{3}{4}\cdot\hat{\eta}(z_{2},\ldots,z_{10})+\sum_{i\in[2,10]}\frac{(i-1)bz_{i}}{10}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[2,10]}\frac{(i-1)bz_{i}}{10}-\biggl(\frac{3z_{2}^{2}}{112}+\frac{3z_{3}^{2}}{56}+\frac{3z_{4}^{2}}{32}+\frac{z_{5}^{2}}{8}+\frac{3(z_{6}+\cdots+z_{10})^{2}}{8}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3}{4}\bigl(z_{5}z_{7}+(z_{4}+z_{5})z_{8}+(z_{3}+z_{4}+z_{5})z_{9}+(z_{2}+z_{3}+z_{4}+z_{5})z_{10}\bigl)\bigg).

The lemma now follows from some elementary but tedious calculations to establish the desired upper bound for
\eta(b,z_{2},\ldots,z_{10})
, which are presented in the Appendix (see Proposition
A.1
).

5.2
\mathcal{A}_{4}
and
\mathcal{C}

In this subsection, we prove the following upper bound for
\alpha\cdot e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{C})
.

Lemma 5.9
.

For every
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C_{\ref{LEMMA:A4-C-bipartite-bound}}=C_{\ref{LEMMA:A4-C-bipartite-bound}}(\alpha)>0
such that

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{C})\leq h_{\alpha}(a_{4},c)+C_{\ref{LEMMA:A4-C-bipartite-bound}}n.

Recall from Section
1.3
that a member
M=\{u_{1},u_{2}\}\in\mathcal{C}
sees a member
Q\in\mathcal{A}
if there exist two vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in Q
such that both
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are adjacent to
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.

Fact 5.10
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{A}
and
M\in\mathcal{C}
. Suppose that
e(Q,M)\geq 5
but
M
does not see
Q
. Then there exists a unique vertex in
Q
that is adjacent to both vertices in
M
, while each of the remaining vertices is adjacent to exactly one vertex in
M
. In particular,
e(Q,M)=5
.

The key ingredient in the proof of Lemma
5.9
is as follows.

Lemma 5.11
.

There do not exist three distinct members
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\in\mathcal{A}_{4}
and two distinct members
M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
such that, for every
i\in[2]
,

(i)

e(Q_{i},Q_{i+1})=15
,

(ii)

\min\left\{e(Q_{i},M_{i}),~e(Q_{i+1},M_{i})\right\}\geq 5
but
M_{i}
does not see
Q_{i}
or
Q_{i+1}
.

Proof of Lemma
5.11
.

Suppose to the contrary that this claim fails. Fix three distinct members
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\in\mathcal{A}_{4}
and two distinct members
M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
such that, for
i\in[2]
,
(i)
and
(ii)
hold.
It follows from
(ii)
and Fact
5.10
that, for each
i\in[2]
, the edges between
M_{i}
and
Q_{i}
(as well as those between
M_{i}
and
Q_{i+1}
) are distributed as follows: there exists a unique vertex in
Q_{i}
(resp.
Q_{i+1}
) that is adjacent to both vertices in
M_{i}
, while each of the remaining three vertices is adjacent to exactly one vertex in
M_{i}
.
For each
i\in[2]
, let
p_{i}
denote the vertex in
Q_{i}
that is adjacent to both vertices in
M_{1}
.

Q_{1}
Q_{2}
Q_{3}
M_{1}
M_{2}
Q_{1}
Q_{2}
Q_{3}
M_{1}
M_{2}

Figure 31:
Auxiliary figure for the proof of Lemma
5.11
Case 1.

Case 1
:
p_{1}p_{2}\in G
.

Let
p_{3}
denote the vertex in
Q_{3}
that is adjacent to both vertices in
M_{2}
(the existence of
p_{3}
is guaranteed by
(ii)
and Fact
5.10
). Since
e(Q_{2},Q_{1})=15
, there exists a vertex
q_{2}\in Q_{2}\setminus\{p_{2}\}
that is adjacent to all three vertices in
Q_{1}\setminus\{p_{1}\}
. Similarly, there exists a vertex
s_{2}\in Q_{2}\setminus\{p_{2},q_{2}\}
that is adjacent to all three vertices in
Q_{3}\setminus\{p_{3}\}
.
However, the rotation shown in Figure
31
would increase the size of
\mathcal{B}
while keeping the size of
\mathcal{A}
unchanged, contradicting the maximality of
\mathcal{B}
.

Q_{1}
Q_{2}
Q_{3}
M_{1}
M_{2}
Q_{1}
Q_{2}
Q_{3}
M_{1}
M_{2}

Figure 32:
Auxiliary figure for the proof of Lemma
5.11
Case 2.

Case 2
:
p_{1}p_{2}\not\in G
.

Similar to the proof of Case 1, let
q_{2}
denote the vertex in
Q_{2}
that is adjacent to both vertices in
M_{2}
(it might hold that
p_{2}=q_{2}
).
Note that, by Fact
5.10
, there exists a vertex
u_{1}\in M_{1}
that is adjacent to at least three vertices in
Q_{1}
, including
p_{1}
. Fix three such vertices (including
p_{1}
), and let
t_{1}
denote the remaining vertex in
Q_{1}
. Since
p_{1}p_{2}\not\in G
and
e(Q_{1},Q_{2})=15=16-1
, the vertex
t_{1}
must be adjacent to all three vertices in
Q_{2}\setminus\{q_{2}\}
. However, the rotation shown in Figure
32
would increase the size of
\mathcal{B}
while keeping the size of
\mathcal{A}
unchanged, contradicting the maximality of
\mathcal{B}
.

This completes the proof of Lemma
5.11
.

We are now ready to present the proof of Lemma
5.9
.

Proof of Lemma
5.9
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Recall that
c=|\mathcal{C}|
. Similar to the proof of Lemma
5.2
, we may assume that
c
is large, say
c\geq 4
.

Define an auxiliary graph
H
whose vertex set is
\mathcal{A}_{4}
and two members
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{4}
are adjacent in
H
iff
e(Q,Q^{\prime})=15
.
Recall from Lemma
3.5

(i)
that
e(Q,Q^{\prime})\leq 15
for every pair
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{4}
, so we have

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{4})\leq 15|H|+14\left(\binom{a_{4}}{2}-|H|\right)+6a_{4}=7a_{4}^{2}+|H|-a_{4}.

(19)

Define an auxiliary bipartite graph
F
whose vertex set is
\mathcal{A}_{4}\cup\mathcal{C}
and a pair of members
(Q,M)\in\mathcal{A}_{4}\times\mathcal{C}
are adjacent in
F
iff
e(Q,M)\geq 5
(recall from Lemma
3.10

(ii)
that
e(Q,C)\leq 5
for all but at most one member
C\in\mathcal{C}
).
Define

\displaystyle Y\coloneqq\left\{Q\in\mathcal{A}_{4}\colon d_{F}(Q)\geq\frac{c+4}{2}\right\}.

It follows from the definition of
Y
and Lemma
3.10

(ii)
that

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\leq|Y|\cdot\left(5(c-1)+2\cdot 4\right)

\displaystyle\quad+(|\mathcal{A}_{4}|-|Y|)\cdot\left(4\cdot\left(c-\frac{c+4}{2}\right)+5\cdot\left(\frac{c+4}{2}-1\right)+2\cdot 4\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{9a_{4}c}{2}+\frac{c|Y|}{2}+5a_{4}-2|Y|\leq\frac{9a_{4}c}{2}+\frac{c|Y|}{2}+5n.

(20)

Claim 5.12
.

The induced graph
H[Y]
is
P_{3}
-free. Thus, by Theorem
2.2
,

\displaystyle|H[Y]|\leq\frac{|Y|}{2}.

Proof of Claim
5.12
.

Suppose to the contrary that there exist a copy of
P_{3}
, say
Q_{1}Q_{2}Q_{3}
, in
H[Y]
.
For each
i\in[2]
, let
N_{i}:=N_{F}(Q_{i})\cap N_{F}(Q_{i+1})
, noting from the definition of
Y
that
|N_{i}|\geq 2\cdot\frac{c+4}{2}-c=4
.
By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{4}
, every member in
\mathcal{A}_{4}
is seen by at most one member in
\mathcal{C}
(otherwise, it would belong to
\mathcal{A}_{2,3}
).
Therefore, by the definition of the graph
F
, for each
i\in[2]
, there are at least
|N_{i}|-1-1\geq 2
members
M\in\mathcal{C}
satisfying
\min\left\{e(Q_{i},M),~e(Q_{i+1},M)\right\}\geq 5
and not seeing
Q_{i}
or
Q_{i+1}
.
So we can greedily select two distinct members
(M_{1},M_{2})\in N_{1}\times N_{2}
such that, for
i\in[2]
,
\min\left\{e(Q_{i},M_{i}),e(Q_{i+1},M_{i})\right\}\geq 5
and
M_{i}
does not see
Q_{i}
or
Q_{i+1}
.
However, this is a contradiction to Lemma
5.11
.

It follows from Claim
5.12
that

\displaystyle|H|\leq\frac{|Y|}{2}+|Y|(a_{4}-|Y|)+\binom{a_{4}-|Y|}{2}=\frac{a_{4}^{2}}{2}-\frac{|Y|^{2}}{2}+|Y|-\frac{a_{4}}{2}.

Combining this with (
19
), we obtain

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{4})\leq 7a_{4}^{2}+\frac{a_{4}^{2}}{2}-\frac{|Y|^{2}}{2}+|Y|-\frac{a_{4}}{2}-a_{4}\leq\frac{15a_{4}^{2}}{2}-\frac{|Y|^{2}}{2}.

Combining this with (
20
), we obtain

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\left(\frac{15a_{4}^{2}}{2}-\frac{|Y|^{2}}{2}\right)+\frac{9a_{4}c}{2}+\frac{c|Y|}{2}+5n

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(|Y|-\frac{c}{2\alpha}\right)^{2}+\frac{c^{2}}{8\alpha}+\frac{15\alpha a_{4}^{2}}{2}+\frac{9a_{4}c}{2}+5n.

Viewing
-\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(|Y|-\frac{c}{2\alpha}\right)^{2}+\frac{c^{2}}{8\alpha}+\frac{15\alpha a_{4}^{2}}{2}+\frac{9a_{4}c}{2}+5n
as a quadratic polynomial in
|Y|
, and noting that
0\leq|Y|\leq a_{4}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot e(\mathcal{A}_{4})+e(\mathcal{A}_{4},\mathcal{C})\leq\begin{cases}7\alpha a_{4}^{2}+5a_{4}c+5n,&\quad\text{if}\quad a_{4}\leq\frac{c}{2\alpha},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{15\alpha a_{4}^{2}}{2}+\frac{9a_{4}c}{2}+\frac{c^{2}}{8\alpha}+5n,&\quad\text{if}\quad a_{4}>\frac{c}{2\alpha}.\end{cases}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
5.9
.

6
Local estimation IV
\colon

e(\mathcal{A}_{6}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})

In this section, we prove improved upper bounds (Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2
) for
e(\mathcal{A}_{6}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})
, refining the upper bound

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{6}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle\leq 8a_{6}^{2}+(7b+5c+2d)a_{6}+20a_{6}+\Psi(b,c,d),

(21)

which is derived from from Lemma
3.11
, Fact
3.1
, and the trivial upper bound
e(\mathcal{A}_{6})\leq\binom{4a_{6}}{2}+6a_{6}\leq 8a_{6}^{2}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exists an absolute constant
C_{\ref{LEMMA:A6BCD-upper-bound-b}}>0
such that

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{6}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})\leq C_{\ref{LEMMA:A6BCD-upper-bound-b}}n+\Psi(b,c,d)+\begin{cases}\frac{15a_{6}^{2}}{2}+(7b+5c+2d)a_{6},&\quad\text{if}\quad a_{6}\leq b+c,\\[5.0pt]
8a_{6}^{2}+\left(\frac{13b}{2}+\frac{9c}{2}+2d\right)a_{6},&\quad\text{if}\quad a_{6}\geq b+c.\end{cases}

Lemma 6.2
.

There exist constants
\mu>0
and
C_{\ref{LEMMA:A6BCD-upper-bound-a}}>0
such that if

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{6})\geq\frac{15a_{6}^{2}}{2}+\mu a_{6}\quad\text{and}\quad a_{6}\geq\frac{3b+2c+d}{2}=\frac{n-4k}{2},

then

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{A}_{6}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{D})\leq 8a_{6}^{2}+(3b+2c+d)^{2}+C_{\ref{LEMMA:A6BCD-upper-bound-a}}n.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
will be presented in Section
6.1
.
Proof of Lemma
6.2
will be presented in Section
6.5
after some necessary preparations.

6.1
Proof of Lemma
6.1

In this subsection, we prove Lemma
6.1
.
The key ingredient in the proof is as follows.

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose that
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{6}
are two distinct members satisfying
e(Q_{1},Q_{2})=16
. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

There is at most one member
T\in\mathcal{B}
satisfying

\displaystyle e(Q_{1},T)\geq 7\quad\text{and}\quad e(Q_{2},T)\geq 7.

(ii)

There is at most one member
M\in\mathcal{M}
satisfying

\displaystyle e(Q_{1},M)\geq 5\quad\text{and}\quad e(Q_{2},M)\geq 5.

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

Let us first prove
(i)
.
Suppose to the contrary that there exist two members
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{B}
such that

\displaystyle\min\left\{e(Q_{i},T_{j})\colon(i,j)\in[2]\times[2]\right\}\geq 7.

Assume that
Q_{i}=\{p_{i},q_{i},s_{i},t_{i}\}
and
T_{i}=\{u_{i},v_{i},w_{i}\}
for

.

that, for each

.

.

(ii)
. Suppose to the contrary that there exist two members

Since

, there exists a vertex in

that has at least three neighbors in

.

, there exists a vertex in

.

, the remaining four vertices in

.

.

.
